ParlaMint-ES_2015-04-15-CD150415.u1	The meeting was resumed. Appearance of the President of the Government to report on the European Council of 19 and 20 March 2015. I give the floor to the President of the Government.
ParlaMint-ES_2015-04-15-CD150415.u2	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, on 25 March, the plenary session was suspended, which was to take place in this House and in which it planned to inform your ladies and gentlemen of the development of the European Council held on 19 and 20 March. The reason for this was the tragedy that happened the day before on the Barcelona-Dusseldorf flight of the Germanwings company that cost the lives of one hundred and fifty people. I would like to take this opportunity to reiterate my deepest condolences and solidarity with the families and friends of the victims. This terrible event took place in the French Alps and the victims were mostly European, mainly German and Spanish. I must say that, on such a difficult and sad occasion as this, the signs of unity and solidarity among Europeans have come out, highlighting how Europe is first and foremost a community of values and affections, on the one hand, through the exceptional effort of the French Government and the French authorities to locate and recover the remains of the victims and the extraordinary generosity of the people of the area to welcome and comfort their relatives; on the other hand, with the close cooperation and collaboration that we have maintained from the beginning by the governments of the most affected countries. I can assure you that at that time, when I was in the area of catastrophe with the President of the French Republic, François Hollande, and the Chancellor of the Federal Republic of Germany, Angela Merkel, I was able to perceive with total clarity the intensity of this collaboration. Ladies and gentlemen, my speech in this House on 25 March was to take place just one week after the savage attack in Tunisia, which claimed twenty-one deaths, two of them Spanish citizens, to whose families I would like to transfer all my support once again. Ladies and gentlemen, I would like to convey a conviction to you. We Europeans will remain united and firm in the face of those who want to attack our democracy and our freedoms. Precisely, a union of freedom, security and justice is one of the five priorities of the European Union's strategic agenda adopted by the European Council last June. From full respect for fundamental rights and the rule of law, to all European values and, given the cross-border dimension of international terrorism, especially jihadist, strong cooperation in the European Union is essential. The threat of terrorism continues to be very present, as demonstrated by this cowardly attack. On 24 February, I had the opportunity to inform this House of the decisions taken by the European Union in connection with the attacks on 7 January in Paris. While the essentials are reflected in the statement of the informal meeting of the Heads of State and Government of the European Union held in Brussels on 12 February, there has subsequently been some important progress which I would like to record today in this House. Member States are making progress and, as a good example, in Spain, the Security Forces and Corps are carrying out an essential task of preventing and persecuting jihadist terrorism. This is demonstrated by the operation carried out on 13 March by the National Police, which allowed the arrest of eight people who formed a jihadist propaganda cell connected with the Islamic State terrorist organization, Daesh. This was the first police operation in which a group of these characteristics was disarticulated in Spain. Later last week, the police of the Autonomous Community of Catalonia arrested eleven people accused of being part of a cell also linked to Daesh. These people had the opposing will to commit a terrorist attack in Barcelona. I would like to congratulate both those responsible and those who have successfully carried them out for both operations. Finally, I would remind you that yesterday the ministerial meeting with the Mediterranean partners of the European Union was held in Barcelona to discuss the review of the European Neighbourhood Policy promoted by Spain and that it allowed for a debate between the European Union and its Mediterranean partners on how to better tackle and combat terrorism, I insist, one of the threats common to the two shores of the Mediterranean. I now turn to the development of the European Council held in Brussels on 19 and 20 March. It was the first ordinary European Council in the Latvian Presidency of the Council following the informal meeting of Heads of State and Government held on 12 February. The European Union has embarked on a new path characterised by economic recovery, overcoming the euro crisis, progress in economic and monetary union, now reinforced by the banking union, and a new political cycle following the elections to the European Parliament last May and the appointment of a new European Commission in November. The annual growth of the eurozone economy in the fourth quarter of 2014 was 0.9 % and 1.2 % across the European Union. In Spain, as your ladies and gentlemen know, it reached 2%. As regards employment and also with data for the fourth quarter of 2014, the annual increase was 0.9 % in the eurozone and 1 % in the European Union. In Spain, as your ladies and gentlemen also know, it was 2.4%. The fact is that the eurozone is maintaining its recovery and for the first time since 2007 the economies of all its Member States are expected to grow in 2015. Moreover, at the end of March, the Organisation for Economic Cooperation and Development raised growth forecasts for the eurozone to 1.4% this year and 2% in 2016. In other words, the European Union has provided itself with stability for the coming years. Following the 2014 European elections, a coalition between the popular, socialist and liberal forces was created within the European Parliament, the investiture of the College of Commissioners and the election of Donald Tusk as President of the European Council has taken place in time and form, and the role of the European Commission has been strengthened, given that Jean-Claude Juncker was indirectly elected by European citizens as President of the Commission as the candidate of the most voted list among which the European political parties presented. In addition, the Commission is linked to the European Council by the five priorities of the Union's Strategic Agenda adopted in June 2014 and to which I have already referred. The debate at the last European Council is precisely about achieving the objectives of this strategic agenda. The conclusions consist of three sections: the union of energy, growth and employment and the European Semester and external relations. Energy union is one of the five priorities of the Union's strategic agenda. It was adopted by the European Council in June 2014 and is also one of the ten priorities of the agenda that the President of the European Commission, Juncker, presented in his investiture speech in the European Parliament. This is an important milestone in European integration and, why not say so, an important success for Spain. The energy union aims to provide the European Union with a secure, sustainable and competitive European energy system, and one of its essential elements is the achievement of a truly functioning and fully interconnected internal energy market. So far and despite efforts to harmonise regulation at European level, the Union still has very fragmented energy markets. This, as I have said many times, is particularly serious for our country. Spain is de facto excluded from the internal energy market. Our electricity interconnection with the rest of the European Union is around 4% and with France, our gateway to the internal market, it reaches only 2.7%, once in June the line opened by Prime Minister Valls and me on 20 February is fully operational. This isolation has very important tangible effects for citizens and businesses, which face a higher energy cost. Citizens also have to make a greater effort to pay for the receipt of light and businesses see their production costs rise with the corresponding loss of competitiveness and therefore of the ability to grow and generate employment. It also means that using renewable energy is more expensive and more difficult in Spain. The problem with renewable energies, ladies and gentlemen, is that they are volatile, that is, that in a day with little sun or little wind they barely produce, well, greater interconnection reduces that volatility. That is why, since I assumed the presidency of the Government, I have made the utmost effort in Europe to correct this situation. At the Barcelona European Council in June 2002, nothing more and nothing less than thirteen years ago, a minimum electricity interconnection target of 10% of installed capacity was set. Progress since then had been limited. Over the past three years Spain has worked hard to make this goal a reality and we have already made significant progress in previous European Councils. Firstly, we have again placed the problem of Spain's energy isolation at the centre of the European debate. As you know, at the European Council in May 2013 which discussed the subject of energy, interconnection is once again set as an objective and the need to complete the internal market in this area is reiterated. The Council considered it a priority to take more decisive action to meet the minimum electricity interconnection target of 10% and, within the framework of projects of common interest to the trans-European networks, to call for further concrete measures and projects with European support. Secondly, in 2014 interconnection was achieved as an important element in the new energy and climate framework 2020-2030. Thus, the October 2014 European Council was decisive. The Council agreed on the framework for action of the European Union in the field of climate and energy until 2030, in which interconnections play a key role. For the first time, the absence of interconnections is recognised as a European problem, which therefore needs European solutions. The link between interconnection and renewable energy is also recognized. For all this, the Commission has committed itself to taking urgent measures to promote specific projects, including financial support for these projects with European funds. Finally, the European Council I am reporting to this House today has provided a new impetus for both energy union and interconnection. The Commission has adopted three communications: a framework strategy for an energy union, achieving the 10% electricity interconnection target, preparing the European electricity network for 2020 and the Paris Protocol and a master plan to combat climate change beyond 2020. In the first, the framework strategy communication, the Commission sets out the five priority axes on which the energy union must be based: energy security, solidarity and trust, a fully integrated internal energy market, energy efficiency as a contribution to the moderation of demand, decarbonisation of the economy and research, innovation and competitiveness. Energy efficiency and decarbonisation are particularly important, in which we have taken steps at national level, such as the implementation of the National Energy Efficiency Fund or vehicle renewal programmes such as PIVE, PIMA Air and PIMA Transport. As is obvious, we also share the goal of energy diversification and security. With regard to research and development, we would have liked a greater weight of this element in the strategy because we believe that the union of energy should be an engine of growth and employment. The interconnection, on the other hand, is not only recognised as an essential element of energy union, but the Commission has, as I have said, published a specific communication. In this communication, the Commission gives interconnection political priority. To increase interconnections to the required 10%, European energy infrastructure planning will be reviewed for the coming years. Key projects will be supported by European funds. Spain will be the main beneficiary of this measure, since it is the country that is the furthest away from meeting the 10% target. In fact, there has already been progress in this regard and there are good options for including soon new interconnection projects between Spain and France through the Pyrenees as one of the European priority projects. In fact, the European infrastructure coordinating body, Entso-e, has already indicated its intention to do so this year. In addition, the conclusions highlight the Madrid Declaration of 4 March, signed by the President of the French Republic, the President of the European Commission and the Heads of Government of Portugal and Spain. I would like to stress that the Madrid Declaration is a major step forward in ending the isolation of the Iberian peninsula from the internal energy market. The declaration establishes a programme of actions and identifies a number of electricity and gas interconnection projects that will have to be initiated and developed as soon as possible, using the accelerated procedures allowing the regulation of trans-European energy networks. I turn to the second paragraph of the Council conclusions before us today. It concerns growth and employment and the European Semester. The boost of employment, growth, competitiveness and investment are priorities, both of the Union's strategic agenda and of the new Commission's agenda. Firstly, the European Council has analysed the economic situation in Europe and also the implementation of the structural reforms undertaken by the Member States. For Spain, the European Commission's winter forecast is that GDP will grow by 2.3% in 2015 and 2.5% in 2016. According to the Commission, this positive trend is mainly due to the progress of domestic demand, which is in turn favoured by an improvement in the labour market, more affordable financing conditions, greater confidence and lower energy prices. The Commission also considers that improvements in job creation will be intensified in Spain in the coming months and that unemployment will fall to 20.7% in 2016, 6 percentage points less than in 2013. In Spain, the latest unemployment figures for March have been very good again. The fall in unemployment recorded in 60,214 people this March was the best in thirteen years. Unemployment is decreasing at an annual rate of 7.2% and employment is growing at the highest rate in recent years. Remember that in March the number of members of Social Security increased by 160,579 people, representing almost 540,000 members more than a year ago. Ladies and gentlemen, behind the coldness of the figures lies the reality that matters most, that of those people with their families, their projects and their desires, who will have the opportunity to carry out. [[Applauds]] As for the public deficit, we are on the right track to meet the target in 2015 and to achieve the compliance value of the Stability and Growth Pact established in 2016. The increase in confidence in the Spanish economy, which is also recognised in the Commission's report, has made it possible for the price that Spain must pay to be financed on the markets to the extent that, for the first time in Spain's history, in the six-month auction of letters last April 7, interest has been negative, at 0.002 %, i.e. investors pay for lending money to Spain. These aren't just numbers, either. Getting better financing conditions means in practice that there will be cheaper money for small and medium-sized enterprises and credits for those who want to start a business or expand what they already have, that is, that they can carry out their initiatives and projects. Ladies and gentlemen, I would also like to refer to the outcome of the studies under the macroeconomic imbalances procedure published by the Commission. The study considers that in Spain there are no excessive imbalances and that the risks arising from them have been reduced compared to last year thanks to the growth of the Spanish economy. It is true that imbalances such as the level of unemployment and high indebtedness persist, but the study adds that significant progress has been made over the course of 2014 in the implementation of the specific recommendations made by the Commission in its day. Ladies and gentlemen, I can only welcome the fact that the European Council has seen positive developments in Spain and that it has ratified the economic strategy for which our Government has been advocating in Europe since the beginning of 2012. Five ideas: budgetary consolidation, reforms at national level, reforms at European Union level, deepening economic and monetary union and stabilising financial markets. The change in the situation is certainly remarkable and is reflected in these reports, but we are aware that much remains to be done, both in Spain and in the European Union as a whole. In our case, in the near future and as the House already knows, the Government, as usual, will present to Brussels its national reform plan and its stability programme, which we will adopt on the next 30th day of this April. In other words, at European level and referring to the field of investment promotion, the Union is finalising the design of the Juncker Plan. In its conclusions, the European Council welcomed the agreement reached at the Ecofin Council on 10 March on the regulation of the European Strategic Investment Fund, which is one of the key elements of the Juncker Plan. Under this plan, as you know, European funds will be used for the granting of guarantees that will reduce both the financing of large infrastructure projects and SMEs projects. The ultimate objective is to mobilise €315 billion. The Government of Spain will participate through the Official Credit Institute to support projects approved in our country. Ladies and gentlemen, the European Council has re-emphasized the importance for growth, employment and investment in Europe of the early conclusion of the agreement on the transatlantic trade and investment partnership between the European Union and the United States. Both sides must make every effort to reach an ambitious global agreement before the end of the year. Spain is working to bring the treaty to an ambitious and balanced conclusion that demonstrates the shared vision and joint commitment of Europeans and Americans. Spain believes that the Treaty, while respecting European values and standards, should contribute to the development of an integrated transatlantic market for the benefit of European consumers, users and businesses and should also strengthen the role of the European Union in the global trading system. That is why I have participated together with other partners in two specific meetings to promote negotiations on this agreement, one in the framework of the G-20 Summit in Australia and the other during last year's European Council. The transatlantic relationship has so far been the backbone of the world economy. The European Union and the United States account for approximately 47% of world GDP and more than a third of trade flows. Moreover, the transatlantic relationship is already intense, as the European Union is the first trading partner in the United States, 17% of its total trade in 2013, and this is the case for the European Union with 14.2 of its global total. The potential for growth is significant, as there is no record of two economies of this size negotiating such ambitious trade and investment liberalization and regulatory convergence. Finally, ladies and gentlemen, I turn to the third section of the conclusions on external relations, which dealt with four issues: the European Neighbourhood Policy, the Eastern Partnership Summit to be held in Riga in May, the crisis in Ukraine and relations with Russia and the situation in Libya. I would like to stress the importance of the Mediterranean dimension of the European Neighbourhood Policy. Spain has been insisting on the need to give greater political attention to the Mediterranean neighbours of the Union. Spain has therefore called for and managed to make reference in the conclusions of the European Council to the following issues: the need for the ongoing review of the European Neighbourhood Policy to ensure the European Union's continued and deep commitment to its eastern and southern partners; the European Council will hold a debate on the southern neighbours in October; and the holding in Barcelona on 13 April of an informal Union ministerial meeting with the Mediterranean neighbours, which the European Council expressly endorsed. This meeting, which you know was held in Barcelona last Monday, has enabled our Mediterranean partners to express their views on the European Neighbourhood Policy, to give greater visibility to the Mediterranean dimension of this policy and to address issues and challenges of common interest, as I pointed out at the beginning. With regard to the Eastern Partnership Summit to be held in Riga on 21 and 22 May, the European Council wished to underline its support for the six eastern neighbours of the Union: Ukraine, Moldova, Belarus, Georgia, Azerbaijan and Armenia, and the need to develop cooperation with these countries. The conclusions have an impact on the principle of differentiation, in line with the aspirations of each of them, and stress the importance of areas such as State consolidation, mobility and contacts between citizens, market opportunities and interconnections. Secondly, and with regard to the situation in Ukraine and relations with Russia, the European Council has re-emphasized that the priority now is for the parties to the conflict to implement the Minsk agreements promptly and fully and to fulfil their commitments. To this end, the European Council has expressed the readiness of the European Union to support the process, in particular with regard to the capacity and means of the Organization for Security and Cooperation in Europe, the OSCE, to monitor and verify the implementation of the Minsk agreements. The paragraph of the conclusions on sanctions against the Russian Federation underlines the need for their duration to be linked to the degree and pace of compliance with the aforementioned Minsk agreements. It is essential for Spain to maintain a united position of the European Union. Russia must make a greater commitment to a peaceful and negotiated solution to the crisis, and we cannot forget that if Russia is part of the problem, it should also be part of the solution. The conclusions reflect the European Union's support for the reform process in Ukraine, and the need for further political and economic reforms to improve the well-being of citizens is transferred to the Ukrainian Government. These were also the messages I sent to the Ukrainian Prime Minister, Arseni Yatseniuk, at the meeting we held before the start of the European Council. Finally, in relation to Libya, the European Council considers that the most important thing at the moment is to achieve an immediate and unconditional ceasefire, and that the various parties reach an agreement to form a government of national unity. The European Council reiterates its support for the work of the United Nations, and commends the work of the Special Representative of the Secretary-General of the United Nations, Bernardino León; recalls also the relationship between the crisis in Libya and immigration, and the tragedy of loss of life at sea, and therefore calls for intensified implementation of the measures agreed by the Council in October 2014 to better manage migration flows, and welcomes the Commission's initiative to present a European migration programme in May. And I'm done, ladies and gentlemen. As a matter of balance, I believe that the European Council of 19 and 20 March has been fruitful, first of all because it has demonstrated the ability of the European Union to concentrate its efforts and to move forward on the issues that form part of its strategic priorities. In this new European political cycle, which began on 1 November, the Union is working to restore the confidence of European citizens, and to focus policies on the main challenges facing our economies and our societies. The Union's agenda should focus on areas where the Union can play a truly decisive role. As President Juncker pointed out, in his investiture speech, the European Union must be bigger and more ambitious in big things, and smaller and more modest in small things. It seems to me that the initiatives of this European Council in matters such as energy union, growth, investment, employment, the crisis in Ukraine or the European neighbourhood policy are in that direction. This European Council has highlighted something that I have always considered fundamental in focusing our country's European policy. Spain must ensure that its national objectives become European priorities and that they can be achieved within the European Union. The progress that this European Council has made in the energy union is an example of this. I would also like to highlight in particular what has been achieved in the Union in the area of taxation. Before the European Council last December, I wrote to the President of the Council, Donald Tusk, raising the need to ensure that taxation continues to be at the heart of European Council meetings and setting out the objectives to be achieved. A few days later, the European Council welcomed the Commission's intention to present a proposal on the automatic exchange of information on tax agreements on the web. Last March, the Commission also adopted a legislative proposal aimed at achieving greater cooperation between Member States on cross-border tax agreements through automatic exchange of information. I therefore welcome the results achieved in this Council and the course the European Union has taken in recent months. Ladies and gentlemen, every time I report to this House on the European Councils, I stress something that I am going to refer to again right now, which is that the integration process is moving forward, perhaps not with the speed that some of us would like, but is moving forward, and that for those of us who believe that a more integrated Europe, economically, socially and politically, will be much better for our fellow citizens, does nothing but increase our hopes in the future. Ladies and gentlemen, thank you very much. [[Applauds]]
ParlaMint-ES_2015-04-15-CD150415.u3	Thank you very much, Mr President of the Government. Firstly, Mr Sánchez has the floor for the Socialist Group.
ParlaMint-ES_2015-04-15-CD150415.u4	Thank you, Mr. President. Ladies and gentlemen, I would like to begin by adding to the words of the President of the Government in memory of the victims of the air tragedy in the Alps, and also by showing the solidarity of the Socialist Parliamentary Group with the families and friends of those victims. Ladies and gentlemen, Mr Rajoy, debates like today are undoubtedly of political significance for the day-to-day majority of Spaniards. You have spoken about European economic policy, you have spoken about energy, Libya, Russia, the free trade agreement between the United States and the European Union, about the Juncker Plan, you have not spoken about the situation in Greece, but I will say something about the situation in Greece. In short, they are all fundamental issues that would merit the prior positioning of this House, the Congress of Deputies, its parliamentary groups and also the Government before the Council was held. I would like to draw attention to this fact, ladies and gentlemen, because once you have given a folder to the amendment of the Rules of Procedure of the Congress, demonstrating your null and void will to revitalize parliamentary life in our country, and since we are at the end of the parliamentary term, I would like to assume before all of you - to make it appear in the "Diary of Sessions", ladies and gentlemen - that if the parliamentary majority during the next parliamentary term changes from blue to red, the commitment of that parliamentary majority will be to hold those debates before the European Councils are held. [[Applauds]] Your honors and the Spaniards deserve it. Nor will we add up to two debates on European councils to such an important debate as the debate on the state of the nation, which is precisely what the Spanish Government did during the debate last March. Your honors and the Spaniards don't deserve it either. Ladies and gentlemen, in my opinion and in my group's opinion over the past three and a half years, if anything has happened in Europe, it has been precisely that Spain has lost weight in the major debates in the European Union. [[Protests]] Yes, there are facts that prove it. We have lost a Vice-President of Competition in the European Commission, and we have achieved a police station with two Vice-Presidents above. After eight months of the first announcement by the Eurogroup Presidency for Mr De Guindos, we do not know what will happen to Mr De Guindos' future today. And the most serious thing in my opinion is that Spain has lost one of the posts traditionally held at the Council of the European Central Bank. By the way, after listening to the governor of the central bank in Spain describe the government's cuts as patriots, there is no doubt that the positive change in monetary policy that has taken place by the European markets and economies of the European Central Bank has nothing to do with the influence of the current government, but rather the opposite. By the way, listening to these kinds of statements by the current governor of the Banco de España is another example of the institutional deterioration of our system by the partisan use made by the People's Party of these institutions. Yesterday, ladies and gentlemen, he was the governor of the Banco de España, before yesterday the director of the Tax Agency, and every day the Spanish Radiotelevision Television. You are the most interventionist government of democracy, you are the right-wing always, Mr Rajoy. Democratic regeneration, having just institutions outside party interests, is vital to improving the democratic quality of our country and to strengthening a just economic recovery. For this reason, ladies and gentlemen, in our opinion regeneration and economic recovery go hand in hand. But I said, ladies and gentlemen, that Spain is not present in the major debates of the European Union, that is true, and it also causes us to listen to those responsible for its government to think about the European day as if they were commentators, analysts outside of Spanish political action. The last example was, in my view, the unfortunate statements made by Minister Margallo, ensuring that a hypothetical Greek exit from the euro would not cause any disturbance. According to the Foreign Minister, ladies and gentlemen, the issuance of bonds and the relaxation of monetary policy would make the euro area circumvent the possible exit of Greece from the euro without any difficulty. This was said yesterday by the Minister for Foreign Affairs of the Government of Spain, and I ask you, Mr Rajoy, where do you leave the Greek citizens? The European Union is not a currency, it is not even just a single market; the European dream is first and foremost a comprehensive, inclusive political project, built on the basis of the social market economy, an area protected by fundamental rights and freedoms, a commitment to social cohesion, and a commitment to solidarity. It is clear, ladies and gentlemen, that the new Commission is giving a positive, albeit insufficient, turn to European economic policy, witnessing a gradual abandonment of the austere policies that are causing so much pain in European society; an austere policies that have proved to be inefficient in economic terms - all that is to be seen is the comparison between the evolution of gross domestic product in the United States and in Europe as a whole - an austere policies that have also impacted, ladies and gentlemen, on the basis of the European project. Some freedoms are being cut in several countries of the European Union, and you have not even mentioned those examples. There is an upsurge in populism, the complete freedom of movement between people - or people, rather - on the European continent, social cohesion has receded, and solidarity has given way to nationalist selfishness. Greece, ladies and gentlemen, has been at the epicenter of all these setbacks; in broad layers of its society a real humanitarian drama is lived without any exaggeration. To face this situation with insensitivity, with remoteness, with indifference, with the dogma of false rigour as the only doctrine, or with the permanent reminder of due penance is the kind of anti-European response that we reject. The departure of Greece from the euro would, ladies and gentlemen, be an absolute failure, it would be for Greece and it would also be for the European Union project. That's our position. That cannot therefore be the response of the Government of Spain, it is certainly not the response of my parliamentary group. Firstly, it is not out of responsibility; the policy is precisely to prevent and avoid foreseeable accidents. Secondly, it is not because of consistency with the policies that we have been proposing and also by promoting at European level, because of coherence with our defence of ambitious public investment policies, because of coherence with the flexibility of the Stability and Growth Pact, of a more expansive monetary policy on the part of the European Central Bank, of tax reforms, especially in the fight against tax evasion. What we are defending, ladies and gentlemen, for the whole of the European Union we are also defending for Greece. Thirdly, for solidarity, a value which the Spanish right clearly lacks. Greece must fulfil its obligations, because they are common obligations, ladies and gentlemen, and the Union must facilitate the support, the means and the flexibility necessary for Greece to fulfil them, with the demand for effort, but also with realism. The first step must be the unequivocal commitment to fulfil them, because it is up to the Greeks themselves that it is their priority to address the profound changes that their country needs. It was, ladies and gentlemen, a Greek right-wing government that deceived the European Union with its country's economic data. [[Applauds]] It was, yes, ladies and gentlemen, Greece itself that allowed an unjust and inefficient tax system to survive, ineffective in pursuing fraud and tax evasion. It is now the Greek Government's duty to deal with these problems and to resolve them. The solution is not in the search for useless confrontations with the European Union, it is not in permanently assigning to others the responsibility for their own failures; nor in hindering and extending negotiations in the face of the objective deterioration of the situation, both in terms of growth and capital movements. And of course there is not the solution in the exacerbation of epic symbols that feed populist narrative, but that bring nothing to the needs of Greek citizens. But the worrying thing about it, returning to Spain, Mr Rajoy, is that in the midst of all this the role of your Government, your own, Mr Rajoy, cannot be more contradictory with what is of interest to Spain, because everything the Union can do for Greece will be beneficial for the south of the European Union. Seek accordingly, Mr Rajoy, allies for our country to be where it should be. Let it be said that Spain and Italy together have contributed more funds to help Greece than Germany itself: 32% of the sum between Spain and Italy compared to 29.15% of Germany. But we have not been in any mini-summit where the Greek crisis has been dealt with, for example on 19 March. Ladies and gentlemen, in the dynamics of European construction over the past 60 years there have been two axes of articulation, one dividing Europeanists from anti-Europeanists, and the other separating progressives from conservatives. It is curious, but the great progress of the European Union throughout history was made with a conservative European Chancellor such as Helmut Kohl, and with two European socialist presidents such as Felipe González and Jacques Delors. Today, ladies and gentlemen, understanding is much more difficult, because European conservatives have become dry neoconservatives, embracing austere theses that liquidate social cohesion by calling into question the social market economy, which is the basis of the European project. Mr. Rajoy, look, if that's true, believe me I'm glad you had a good relationship with Mrs. Merkel. I am glad; but I wonder whether such a good relationship should serve only to place Mr De Guindos at the forefront or as president of the Eurogroup. [[Rumors]] Yes, because, on what basis does your affinity with Mrs Merkel rest, Mr Rajoy? The German Chancellor presides over a European creditor state with an unemployment rate of 5%, and you preside over a debtor country with a rate of more than 20% unemployment. Would it not therefore be appropriate, Mr Rajoy, to tell Mrs Merkel that as long as the eurozone has an inflation close to zero it will make it difficult to recover from indebted economies like ours? I'd tell you, Mr. Rajoy. Would it not be appropriate for you to tell Germany that it must boost domestic private consumption in order to improve our exports? I'd tell you, Mr. Rajoy. Would it not be appropriate, Mr Rajoy, to remind Mrs Merkel that she is making more of a point of growth of gross domestic product by consolidating public accounts in our country than all the contrary policies of adjustment to the welfare state, and to science and innovation in our country? And that under the neocon cry of not being able to live above our possibilities you have achieved that there are many Spaniards who live below our needs? I'd tell you, Mr. Rajoy. Your affinity with Mrs Merkel has nothing to do with Europe: it is purely and simply ideology. The chancellor, ladies and gentlemen, commends the advanced student; and you, applied and willing, are grateful even if the interests of the majority of Spaniards are opposed to those defended by the German chancellor. [[Several Members: Oh!-Applause]] You, Mr Rajoy, claim that citizens assume that the economy and well-being are incompatible, and that is not true; that it is not possible to enjoy economic development and to maintain the rights achieved in health, education, and the labour market, but that is not true. You tell us that if we want a recovery this has to be unfair, a recovery in exchange for giving up the dignity of a good job and a good salary. They want citizens to resign, to conform to a Europe and to a Spain of inequalities: an aspiration proper to their ideology, but which is contrary to the European project. Ladies and gentlemen, if we want to strengthen, both in Spain and in Europe, the pact between generations, what we have to do is to strengthen our welfare state, which you are far from doing. Is it necessary to stress, ladies and gentlemen, that the best ally of the intergenerational pact is the educational system and the social lift it sets in motion? That the best pact between generations makes it possible for the sick to depend on the healthy, the old of the young, the unemployed on whom he is employed? To the extent that you drive your demolition, you fatten that inequality in which you have never focused your intervention, Mr Rajoy, by erasing the pact between generations at Spanish and European level. Look, you have referred to the good data that the International Monetary Fund published yesterday, and believe me, Mr Rajoy, if I tell you that we are happy with the improvement in the growth forecasts of our economy, you know that we hate the Montoso doctrine, but you will also understand, Mr Rajoy, that we have serious doubts that you are responsible for the fall of oil, even if welcome is the improvement of the economic environment. However, the International Monetary Fund also warns that the medium-term growth potential of developed economies will not recover pre-crisis levels, warns that productivity growth cannot be expected to accelerate with the current policies, yours, which you have chosen, Mr Rajoy. Without an increase in public investment it will not be possible to recover pre-crisis growth and employment rates. In addition, the fund in another recent report concludes that inequality, the income gap, is narrowing the stronger collective bargaining. This is what the International Monetary Fund says: inequalities are reduced the stronger the collective bargaining, Mrs Báñez; the more the population is formed, Mr Wert, and the stronger the welfare state. It is exactly, Mr Rajoy, quite the opposite of what you are doing from La Moncloa. If we want to take advantage of the tailwind that the improvement of the economic environment brings, then we have to support the whole of the European Union in the purchase of debt in secondary markets that the central bank is doing, and in a transitional way we will have to push up prices and wages in order to facilitate consumption and reduce public debt, which is fundamental in our country, and not abandon the horizon of debt sharing, which is precisely what you are no longer talking about. Mr Rajoy, we welcome a single energy market, a common energy policy that decisively fights climate change, that eradicates energy poverty - which you did not talk about in your speech, and which affects 54 million Europeans -. In our view, this should be the fight thanks to the boost of energy efficiency that will reduce consumption by 40% by 2030, and increase consumption of renewables to 45% by 2030. That is our proposal and not the non-binding agreement of 27 %, ladies and gentlemen, for the Member States adopted in the Council last October. My group is committed to 20% of the Juncker Plan's resources for investment in energy efficiency. Ladies and gentlemen, we welcome the announced interconnections, but we would like, Mr Rajoy, to make the 10% target for 2020 and 15% for 2030 binding, and it is not binding today. I will also tell you that the Juncker Plan is well oriented towards what we believe is the key to growth, which is productive investment, but it is also - let us admit - insufficient the amount earmarked for the Juncker Plan. Of course, we still do not know what Spain's priorities are, what projects, what our contribution will be, the role of the ICO, coordination with the European Investment Bank or the role of the financial sector. With the exception of the expansion of the capital of the European Investment Bank, there are also no contributions of fresh money, and you announced a few days ago that we will devote 1.5 billion euros, much less than the 8 billion euros that Germany, France and Italy are going to allocate. On the content of the Juncker Plan, the European Fund for Strategic Investments, in our view, should not select projects with exclusively cost-effectiveness criteria, but should also have criteria that place those receiving countries, especially those with high unemployment rates, such as Spain, above 20%. I would like to know whether or not you agree with this position and whether you proposed it at the European Council. In any case, I would like to point out that this expenditure should not be calculated for deficit purposes, and I ask Mr Rajoy to say whether he has raised it in Brussels, for the moment we do not know anything about it. For Spain, it would be a priority from the point of view of meeting the deficit targets, because we have a deficit of 5.7% of gross domestic product, Mr Montoro. So you, Mr Rajoy, should not boast of such budgetary rigour, because your rigour is always at the expense of others. We have just heard, ladies and gentlemen, that despite all the cuts in rights, the increase in public debt, we have barely cut the public deficit since you came to the government. Yes, Mr Rajoy, between 2010 and 2011, in full economic recession and with a much higher risk premium than the current one, the previous government reduced the government deficit by 2.1 points of gross domestic product. In the last two years, with much more favourable external conditions and a good dose of accounting engineering from the Minister of Finance, you have only reduced the public deficit of the General Administration of the State by 9 tenths, and have made rest all the effort of fiscal consolidation in the autonomous communities and in the local entities. But, ladies and gentlemen, for the first time in five years the structural deficit has increased, Mr Montoro, and the forecasts for this year point to a further deterioration of the structural balance. With these figures, Mr Rajoy, how can you boast of budgetary rigour? It will be the autonomous communities and the municipalities, but you cannot; you cannot boast of that budgetary rigour. With regard to the negotiations between the United States and the European Union, my group is clearly committed to free trade, but always respecting European environmental, labour and welfare standards. My group has called for impact reports in our country, as other countries have done, and for the Government to appear in the Courts after each round of negotiations has been closed. We need to give transparency to the negotiation, and the Commission has made it clear that it is up to the Member States, in this case the Government of Spain, to make an effort to communicate the status of the TTIP negotiations; that is, it is you, Mr Rajoy, your Government, who has to appear in this Congress to explain the status of those negotiations. There are encouraging developments in the fight against tax fraud at European level: the problem of multinational tax planning, transparency in tax agreements between companies and national authorities, harmonisation of corporate taxes, more ambitious financial transaction tax or evaluation of the new savings directive. Honestly, ladies and gentlemen, I believe that Spain should lead that agenda together with the European Commission. A few weeks ago my parliamentary group registered a proposal for a tax to avoid the diversion of profits by multinationals, especially by multinationals, as the United Kingdom proposes - by the way, there they call it Google tax - and I encourage you, Mr Rajoy, to review it and to work together in an active way to combat tax evasion and avoidance in our country. Unfortunately, the Spanish Government cannot lead the fight against fraud and transparency in Europe because it simply cannot set an example, Mr Montoro, either because the political direction of the Tax Agency is more likely to justify than to pursue the fiscal fraud of the People's Party, or because there are serious doubts - in my opinion there are - about whether it is proper or improper for Minister Montoro to constitute a firm that is the paradigm of the revolving door, or at the same time; but the truth is that you are fighting tax fraud, no lesson, about tax amnesty, all; all; all. [[Applauds]] By the way, Mr Rajoy, in Rodrigo Rato had Mr Montoro a great teacher to talk about tax amnesties and apply them. I am finishing, ladies and gentlemen, by making a mention of Ukraine and Libya. Since the Minsk II process is developing, although imperfectly, it does not seem that this is the time to increase sanctions against Russia, although the existing ones should be maintained until Moscow's attitude to conflict resolution is effective. With regard to Libya, I would like to express our solidarity and the horror that has caused us to know about the tragedy that happened yesterday in the Mediterranean with 400 immigrants killed on the Italian coasts, which shows that we urgently need to resolve the crisis in Libya. Ladies and gentlemen, Mr Rajoy, I conclude. I end with a paradox, because it is paradoxical that you defend in Brussels policies such as the Juncker Plan, which in the end are incentives to aggregate demand, when you in Spain do just the opposite. The Juncker Plan has nothing to do with the policies that the Government of Spain has been implementing in our country for three years, a policy that has driven away job-creating investment and favored job-creating disinvestment through labour reform designed for easy dismissal and precarious recruitment. The above, ladies and gentlemen, is clear - for example only - in investment in R & D+i. Spanish expenditure on innovation was 1.24% of gross domestic product in 2013 compared to the European average of 2%, or above, well above 2% in some cases, such as Germany. A further test of the path taken by his Government, an unjust recovery that harms precisely the majority of Spaniards; a recovery at the expense of dismantling the welfare state and dismantling and dismantling the engines of future economic growth. A fair economic recovery will only be possible, ladies and gentlemen, with an Executive that governs for the majority and that, Mr Rajoy, is not so for you. Thank you so much. [[Applauds]]

The disconnection, of course, is a problem to overcome, but not the one that is at the root of the shortcomings of the Spanish electricity market. In this sense, there is one worrying thing. You say that we are finally going to have a European energy market. Okay, but what's it gonna be like? Because since the model is Spanish, of course, it's to start shaking. More stuff. Therefore, what we are asking the Government of Spain, which you represent at these Council meetings, to make a genuine, sincere and effective effort to demand more transparency and more information for the public and for the participation of civil society and economic and social actors involved in a negotiation that pursues an interesting objective, but which can achieve a completely opposite effect to what is being pursued: a rejection of economic integration in Europe and a rise in isolationist, economic-type nationalist discourses, which your Government, of course, also represents very well. This is creating a tension that threatens the future of a European project that has always been accompanied by the goal of social integration, which is nothing more than creating European citizenship. It is not a question of pursuing objectives contrary to capitalism or such slogans that we certainly do not share, but of ending the European nationalist discourses of governments once and for all, that what they are doing is transferring to public opinion the poisonous, poisoned idea that local economic problems are due to European policies and local economic successes are due to government policies. As you know very well, the timid economic progress that is taking place in Spain with regard to the exit from the crisis owes more to the policies of the European Bank and to certain European decisions than to national policies. When you also boast of Spain's great successes in the fight against unemployment, you always forget to say that that is very good, creating new jobs and increasing, for example, social security affiliations, but it serves very little if we move away from the European economic model to get closer to the Chinese. A work model is being fostered that is of course unsustainable. If you tell us that it is a first step towards a return to an economic model based on quality employment, it will seem very good to us, but we see self-satisfaction and that is a little the objective: that Spain looks more like Morocco or China than Denmark or Austria. And that is a mistake that our group does not share and we do not want to be accomplices to such a decision. And the TTIP, exactly the same. But as interaction between States and their mutual duties increases, the area of those to whom political decisions themselves must be justified is broadened to the extent that they are significantly affected. Precisely the failure of the Union - Innerarity continues - to solve the current economic crisis is due to the gap between the political instruments and the nature of the problems, to which the States have been unable to internalize the consequences of interdependence, continue to impose externalities on each other and are unable to regulate the transnational forms of power that escape their control.
