In any case, the only thing we have done is what is being done throughout the history of Spain, who governs, when there were debates of these characteristics, which have been many, which does not mean that a criterion cannot be changed, but does not mean that it cannot be maintained, because things have not worked badly. In any case, as some other speaker has also raised, I am prepared to speak on this matter. A few comments have been made on the weight and influence in Europe to which I have to respond, more than anything so that they are not left simply in the "Diary of Sessions" and someone can interpret that I have given my acquiescence to these statements. We've lost a vice president. In fact, neither France, nor Germany, nor the United Kingdom, nor Spain, nor the major countries of the European Union have a vice-presidency, what they have are portfolios with content. Spain has the Energy portfolio, which previously had Germany, and also the Climate Change portfolio, which previously had Denmark, and then there are some vice presidents, but the portfolios have France, Germany, the United Kingdom and Spain. Reference has also been made to the Governor of the Banco de España. I am not going to refer to the former governor of the Banco de España, who was talking about the time when labor reform and issues that had nothing to do with his own competences, which came from the Secretariat of State for Finance, but I am going to say that he did something that if he had not done so he would have avoided many problems in the future, because it is no longer a matter of declarations. Probably, if as a regulator we had been alert to credit performance in Spain, we would have saved ourselves much of the very serious external debt problems that we have suffered over the years. [[Applauds]] But, finally, let us leave history on its site, even if this is the subject to which I have just referred to a subject which has caused many problems for all the Spaniards in the future and which is one of the issues which has undoubtedly contributed to the very serious economic crisis from which fortunately we have already begun to emerge. To talk about the influence in Europe of the Spanish Government on dates such as those we are living in, I think it makes little sense at all to say that the influence of the Spanish Government in Europe has increased over the last few years. I say this because a meeting was held here on 4 March, attended by the President of the French Republic, the President of the Government of Portugal, the President of the European Investment Bank and the President of the European Commission to discuss one of the most important issues for Europe and Spain, such as the energy debate, which has been discussed so many times here this morning. I believe that this shows, among other things, that the Spanish Government has sufficient influence to bring this matter to the European Councils, to hold a meeting here at the highest level and to make public commitments. There is also little point in talking about this loss of influence when, on Monday of the same week, 32 out of 36 foreign ministers from the European Union and from the northern Mediterranean in Africa attended a meeting in Barcelona dealing with issues that are also central to the European Union's agenda, the issue of jihadist terrorism, for example, the issue of emigration and the unfortunate events and events to which many spokesmen have referred and to which I will also refer in the rest of my speech. In the same way, it makes little sense when, after taking possession of its responsibilities, both the President of the European Council and the President of the Commission have come to Madrid, the first, to deal with issues affecting the Mediterranean, which are very important for the European Union, when he has not yet gone to other major Mediterranean countries. In fact, I will tell the ladies and gentlemen that the influence comes basically from doing things right. I came to the government at the end of 2011, but it was 2012 when I attended European Council meetings for the first time and I saw what was happening then with our country and I see how our country is being treated today. If you - it is a very important issue - see Spain's assessment in the documents of the European Semester, you will be able to see how things have changed and that when you win influence, credit and respect is when you do things right and not when you do them wrong. I do not want to exhaust them, but there are some aspects that should be left in the minutes of the meeting we are holding today. In the European Semester 2012, when we arrived at the Government, the Commission concluded that Spain was experiencing very serious imbalances - that is to say, with these words - which had to be resolved urgently. Three years later, in the report for 2015, it considers that progress has been made in adjusting imbalances and that the resumption of growth has reduced risks. In these conclusions of the European Commission, Spain is better valued than France, Italy or Portugal, and I will not go into any more details because they do not do so. As for the macroeconomic situation, in 2012 it was said: The high indebtedness of the private sector and an unprecedented level of unemployment continue to obscure the prospects for Spain. The situation has been aggravated by the rigid labour market and the low wage adjustment. In 2015, the outlook is very different. The Commission points out that, after three years of recession, growth resumed in 2014 and prospects for 2015 and 2016 are improving, domestic demand driven by private consumption continues to propel growth and investment was one of the main factors for recovery. On the external sector and competitiveness, in 2012 it was said: The high external debt - a problem I referred to earlier when I spoke about the former governor of the Banco de España - poses serious risks and must lead to a sustainable debt. In 2015, the report on this issue says: The current account balance has been reversed, from a maximum deficit of 9.6% of GDP in 2007 to a surplus of 1.5% in 2013. Thus, the adjustment of the current account balance led the economy to regain its net financing capacity vis-à-vis the rest of the world. As for private sector debt, in 2012 it was said: The large accumulated private sector debt is very worrying. The magnitude and extent of the necessary deleveraging implies a reduction in domestic demand in the medium term. Economic growth is therefore expected to be weak, with adverse implications for the debt repayment capacity of households and businesses. This was said in 2012 and they were right, as we saw in 2012 and 2013. However, in 2015 they say: Households and businesses are reducing their over-indebtedness. In addition, aggregate deleveraging is taking place selectively with a financial sector willing to finance healthier companies with favourable growth prospects. The Commission considers that the process of decoupling in Spain has so far been successful. Well, we could talk about what they say about the labour market, 2012: Unemployment has reached an unprecedented level and employability and segmentation of the labour market are significant bottlenecks. In 2015 they say that unemployment remains very high, which is true, but the trend has changed completely. There is an improvement in labour market conditions supported by a revival of the economy and a moderate rise in wages. We could talk about what they say in education and training policies and then it also has some negative aspects, such as when they say that we have not done the law of professional services, that there has been no progress, or when they refer to the mechanisms of control of the autonomous and local administrations, but anyone who compares with a minimum of objectivity what the European Commission said about Spain in the first half of 2012 and what it says today, in the first half of 2015, can only acknowledge that there has been a change in our country, fortunately to be better, although we still have a lot to do. Therefore, I cannot accept - and that is why, as I said earlier, I am referring to this part of your speech, Mr Sánchez - that we talk about the weight and influence of Spain as opposed to saying that it has improved with regard to previous situations. There is a different matter than that which I did not refer to in my speech, plainly and plainly because it was not spoken of or, rather, as I think Mr Sostres recalled in his speech, only for forty-seven seconds, which is the situation on Greece. The only thing that was said about Greece by the President of the European Council was that there was a meeting, but that meeting was not going to change at all, as it could not be otherwise, the decisions that had been taken in the Eurogroup. Forty-seven seconds was what was said about Greece, because it was not on the agenda and because it was a topic that was being dealt with who has the competence to do so, which is the Eurogroup, at that time. Having said that, as all speakers have spoken of Greece, I also feel obliged, because it is also an important issue, to speak of Greece. I will try, as clearly as possible, to express my opinion on what has happened and what may happen in the future. The first thing I want to say is that I do not want Greece to leave the euro. I think it is bad, first of all for Greek citizens, it is bad for the rest of the countries of the European Union and it is bad for the whole of the European project. Secondly, I want to be in solidarity with Greece and in fact my party has voted here - and the vast majority of this House too - all the decisions that have been taken on Greece, the first, the granting of a credit in 2010 of almost 7 billion euros that the government that presided over Mr Rodríguez Zapatero presented and that we support. We therefore want Greece to remain in the euro, we want to be in solidarity with Greece, but we also want Greece to fulfil its commitments as others do. I have to say in connection with some intervention that has taken place here that the position that the Spanish Government has maintained is exactly the same as that which the rest of the European governments have maintained regardless of their ideology, regardless of their ideology. That is to say, anyone who says that in the Eurogroup we have maintained a different position from the rest of the European governments does not tell the truth. From there, what has happened in Greece in the last few days? Greece had a program, it was a country that lived under program because it had been rescued. The program ended in November last year. What was being discussed was whether or not Greece had fulfilled its commitments. If Greece fulfilled its commitments, it would receive the last aid, EUR 7.2 billion, half of the International Monetary Fund, the rest of the European Central Bank and the MED. As elections were convened in Greece, it was decided that this evaluation would be extended and not done until the end of February. In this situation, a new Government accepted the responsibility to lead Greece's destinations. The new government said it was not going to meet its commitments, demanded unconditional money from the rest of its European partners, called for a World War II repair conference, announced that it would back off some of the measures the previous government had taken and announced an increase in public spending against its commitments. In the meantime, Greece has to pay its debts. You don't have to pay your debts to Europe. Mr Coscubiela spoke here about debt restructuring. The debt that Greece has with Spain is a debt whose principal begins to be paid in thirty years, that is, we can restructure or not, it can start within fifty or one thousand, but it starts to pay in thirty years, and the interest within ten, that is, the loan of 7,000 million and the guarantees to the MEDE of 18 billion that Spain has makes us talk about something that is for thirty years and the interest for ten years. It is true that he has to pay to the International Monetary Fund - and in fact he made a payment last week - and also to the European Central Bank, but his most important creditors are the European partners, we want to continue helping him and we are not going to tell him that what he has to start paying in thirty years' time he is paying now. What we will have to say to him is that he fulfils his commitments. On 20 February this year, Greece, realizing that Europe had to maintain what we had all agreed together, says it accepts conditionality, that it is going to meet budgetary objectives, that it is not going to reverse the measures of the previous government, that it is going to talk to the troika, although it is now called otherwise but they are exactly the same interlocutors, and that it is going to bring about reforms. He is told that this is a step forward, Greece is asked to implement those reforms, and the situation we are in now is exactly that. If Greece implements those reforms - and I think there will be flexibility on the part of everyone, of course I am in favour of that - Greece will give an important signal of seriousness, of compliance with commitments and I believe that we can start a positive path. That is the situation in Greece right now, and that is what the Spanish Government thinks, which is exactly what the rest of the governments think. Having said that, I believe that the main problem Greece has in my opinion is the need to grow and create jobs. He has no problem paying debt, because, as I said earlier, the bulk of the debt is owed to the European countries and to the MEDE and the interest is not beginning to be paid until ten years from now. So much so that I say that Greece, in proportion to its GDP, pays less interest, for example, than Spain, Italy or Ireland. There are probably very few countries in the Union that pay less. Once I have finished this chapter of Greece, on which many of the honourable Members have taken an interest, I would like to make a few brief comments on some of the statements that have been made regarding economic recovery and some economic lessons. Naturally, I tend to what everyone says, but there are some who by their trajectory do not have too much moral authority to give economic lessons, because I have arrived here and I have met a country on the verge of bankruptcy, on the verge of rescue, with a risk premium for the air that came to be above 600 points, I have encountered that there were public institutions in Spain, among others, autonomous communities, that could not go to the markets, I have encountered all the imbalances that existed and because there was, the public deficit, the deficit of the foreign sector, the financial system as it was, a negative economic growth for several years and a job creation that was not such, because every day that happened more employment was destroyed. Some things have changed. We are now growing to 1.4 last year and this year we are going to be the euro area country that we are growing most clearly. We're already creating jobs. Then they stick to everything, but after seven years of job destruction, last year 440,000 jobs were created and this year will be more, and this year in March 160,000 people have been able to join Social Security. It's positive data. I know there's a lot left to do, but, man, let's not give any lessons on these matters. There's talk of inequality. Well, the 80/20 index, the Gini index and the Arope index began to improve for the first time in many years in 2013; we still don't have the data for 2014, but you will see how much better they are. These are positive facts and I understand that the opposition often has to criticize because it does, but the issues could be chosen a little better. Just like when we talk about the welfare state. To maintain and improve the welfare state there is something that is crucial and that everyone understands, you have to create employment, and who makes decisions that generate in a legislature the loss of 3,400,000 jobs should not give lessons on this issue, because that led to the loss of 70 billion euros of revenue - that is what the whole House knows - and, of course, if there is 70 billion euros less income, it is not so easy to handle things. [[Applauds]] We still handled them, because the pension system is there, and this party has never frozen pensions. The health system is there, and there are 800,000 more Spaniards with a health card now than at the end of 2012. They have therefore had to make decisions, but this is a country that can now be proud of its welfare model. I am going to make some comments on energy that have naturally occupied the vast majority of the speakers. I am pleased that Mr Sánchez is in favour of the single energy market - I did not think otherwise - and that he is concerned about energy poverty, that he is concerned about the situation of renewables, energy efficiency and that he agrees with 10% of interconnection. That's fine with me. I am not going to go into any more details, because we have already had a debate on this matter here. However, yesterday the INE gave the light price data for this quarter, the first of this year, and there was a decrease in the electricity price above 2%; last year, 2014, there was also it; the only year in which there was a significant increase was in 2012, when we had just arrived at the government. Then, when I answer other speakers, I will comment on the situation of renewable energy, because I think it is important that I do. On the objectives of the deficit, another matter to which Mr Sánchez also referred in his speech, hearing this makes it difficult for me, because I came to the government at the end of 2011 and they told me: in 2011 we will have a deficit of 6 and suddenly it was 9 -3 points more - and the one who had to face, as was natural, for that was the president of the government, it was me. We are negotiating with the European Union, we have been reducing the deficit and it has been very difficult, because reducing the deficit with two years in recession is not easy. This year we had the target of 5.8%, which was set by the European Union, and we said: we believe that we can reach 5.5 - we were ambitious -; in the end it was 5.7 but it was motivated by the judgment of the Court of Strasbourg on the health cent, which gave us a tenth longer than GDP; if not, we would be at 5.5, although the Commission's objective was 5.8. Therefore, it does not seem fair to me that you are now talking about the public deficit, not because of your history and your party, nor because of what has happened over these three years in our country. Free trade agreement. It is an important issue to which many of the speakers have referred. There is no problem in appearing in the Courts, and I will therefore give instructions for such appearance to take place as quickly as possible. As far as immigration is concerned, I certainly agree with what the vast majority of the people who have spoken here have said. Yesterday in Italy more than 400 people lost their lives. It is a dramatic event because life is the most important thing people have, and it adds to everything that is happening over the last few days in that country. While I was in Barcelona last Monday, also near the coast of Libya, destined for Italy, 9 people lost their lives. Then I will go into more detail on some issues related to this policy. I shall now reply to Mr Duran's speech, which began by talking about Greece, and then entered the Council's three subjects: energy, employment and foreign policy. My position on the subject of Greece is sufficiently clear: we want Greece to remain in the euro - I repeat - we believe that it is good for everyone, we need to make an important effort for Greece to remain in the euro, but when someone is in a club and voluntarily assumes rules of play it is essential that those rules of play comply with them, because if we do not end up having a problem, and if every time there is a change of government in a certain country of the European Union the new government says that it is going to do the policies that it considers appropriate and appropriate, I could have said that it was not responsible for the public deficit that was in 2011, and that it was not going to come down, which, as anyone can understand, is somewhat absurd. I would like to refer to an issue that other speakers have also taken up in their speech, and that is why we were not at that meeting. That meeting was organized by Mr Tsipras and only invited the presidents of France and Germany - it was he who invited - not the president of Italy who was not at that meeting. That meeting did not have as you all know the value of any kind, and as I pointed out earlier in the forty-seven-second speech on this matter from Mr Tusk at the European Council, so it was made known to the people who were there, that we were the heads of State and Government of the different countries of the Union. I agree primarily with all of the speeches you have made on energy, but I would like to make a few comments, not many, because I have already referred to some things in the reply to Mr Sánchez. I agree that this is not just an economic issue; that is to say, this has political significance, and you said well when you brought back what is happening in Ukraine and Russia: simply with the interconnection of gas in Spain, 40% going from Russia to the rest of the countries of the European Union could have been sent, and therefore could have bought gas from Algeria from the rest of the countries of the European Union. I think that's a fact that says it all and corroborates the statement you've made here. This is therefore an important issue from all points of view, and it affects, for example, even immigration: if there are good electricity interconnections, electricity can be produced, and there are those who are willing to invest in North Africa, which is good for those countries and good for the whole of the European Union that would have more sources of supply. I therefore agree with what you have just said, and indeed all of us, Europe, would be more sovereign. There is another issue on which I would also like to make a brief comment, and it is the subject of energy efficiency. If we have done things in Spain, and in fact we have approved a National Energy Efficiency Action Plan 2014-2020 that will allow Spain to meet the savings objectives that derive from the directive, and very recently the Ministry of Industry itself, through the Institute for Diversification and Energy Saving, has allocated 168 million euros for energy efficiency and saving projects, with four lines of action: the energy rehabilitation of buildings, transport, SMEs and the large enterprise in the industrial sector and outdoor lighting, and the savings produced by these programs amount to the annual consumption of more than 150,000 families, which will avoid the annual emission to the atmosphere of more than 440,000 tons of CO2. Therefore, this is an issue that concerns us, it is an issue that we have devoted ourselves to, as is our obligation, and it is an issue on which we will continue in the future. I now turn to the subject of employment, not without saying that in energy policy there are two issues which are crucial. The first, the interconnections. They are a real problem for us. Before we talked here - I think it was Mr Martínez Gorriaran - about the price. Yes; if at a certain point in Spain it doesn't rain or it doesn't rain and we don't have interconnections, we have to have what we have, which are gas cycles, combined cycles that are not working normally, but which we have to have, because if we don't run out of energy. If we had the interconnection we would import it without any problem, and Spain could also export energy, because at the moment it produces much more electricity than it consumes. This issue is therefore important, and interconnections are the first priority, at least from my point of view, to achieve the great objective of lowering the price of light. Mr Duran also referred in his speech to employment, budgetary consolidation and reforms. He has talked about investment, which needs to be done and prioritized for the quality of projects that generate employment and growth. I agree, but I am not with any of the other statements you have made regarding high-speed trains. Luckily in Spain we have a good high speed network and that has been very good, because it has improved tourism, improved movements, investments in cities, and indeed in the vast majority of them - you know better those of Catalonia - are very happy to have there the high speed train. On the agreement with the USA you say that it should end in Obama's time. That's the goal. Obama ends his term as everyone knows at the end of 2016. The idea is that this can be closed at the beginning of 2016. As I said earlier, I am prepared to give rise to a debate on this matter in this House. I have to say that I am in favour of the free trade agreement with the United States. This is fundamental, the world goes that way. To oppose free trade does not know if it would make sense five centuries ago; it is now thought of by very few. We are the two big areas of the world where you trade most. We have a very important part of world GDP, we can set rules for all, and at this time the most important problem there is, the key, the decisive one, refers to the difficulties in resolving investor disputes, that is, whether or not arbitration mechanisms exist. Various speakers have spoken on this subject. There's been talk of multinationals saying there are multinationals who don't want to. This is really going to be very positive for small and medium-sized enterprises in Europe, and also for those in the United States. Here whenever it comes to opposing someone you have to give someone else a stake. No, some multinationals are against this free trade agreement. In foreign policy, the issue of Ukraine and Russia is certainly complex and very difficult to resolve. We also have to deal with it as it has been said here - I think it was Mr Esteban in his speech -: one thing is the Russia-Ukraine problem, another thing is how Ukraine works, what it has to do and what it does not have to do. I believe that at the moment the solution that has been adopted and the position that the European Union maintains is the most sensible. That is to say, at the moment it is a question of compliance with the Minsk agreements, even if only for two reasons: because they are the only agreements that exist, and because they have led to a ceasefire, with exceptions, that there are, but that is already a positive fact in itself. We know very well the situation in the agricultural and livestock sector - up to that point we could get there - and last year we worked in the European Union, before the Commission, for compensation and we are prepared for anything that might happen in the future. I turn to the subject of Libya and take the opportunity to speak briefly about immigration policy, because it is the position I have always held, since I was Minister of the Interior a few years ago and I had to attend JHA meetings in Brussels. Libya is indeed a failed State today. Some intervener - I think it was Mr Esteban - was saying: this of them agreeing for a government of national unity everyone knows that this is not going to happen. We'll see about that. I don't think - and I don't know anyone who has - of a different alternative. We are working intensively, the UN together with the European Union, we have the same person at the head of operations, and at the moment what needs to be done is to try to have a government of national unity. What cannot be there are two parliaments, two governments and only one bank in Libya, which is the only thing that works there, I suppose. This is a subject that affects many things, including immigration. The issue of immigration is crucial. I do not know whether, as Mrs Barkos said in her speech, it is necessary to put it at a level above jihadism. I think we have to deal with both things at once, because they are certainly important issues, but the solution is ultimately to agree with the countries of origin and transit where these people come from, and to fight against the mafia and improve cooperation. I agree with what has been said, that the main problem in these countries is poverty, probably along with human rights. We would certainly be in an infinitely better situation if we were to make progress in those two matters - others would come later. Therefore, Mr Duran, I agree with what you have said about immigration, and that this is not a religious war, but between civilization and barbarism, and in fact many Muslims have also been killed by these groups, which are still fanatical and terrorist groups. I shall now reply to Mr Coscubiela's speech. I have explained my position on the subject of Greece, which was the first thing he was concerned about in his speech. I told him about the debt restructuring. I have pointed it out here, but I insist on what I have pointed out to you before. The problem is economic growth and employment. I have explained how the Greek debt situation is, and I think it is best for everyone to have an understanding between the Eurogroup, which ultimately represents - because they are there - all the countries of the European Union, and the Greek Government. He's made a number of statements. On energy issues there is one issue that I think is good for the whole House to know. We came to the government with a broken system, because there were 26 billion euros of debt that each year were increasing by 7,000 or 8,000 more, and we had to do something. If this resulted in a fall in the price of light, it turns out that in the last eight years the light had increased by 6.9%. Either we did something or we had a problem. Why did this happen? For many reasons, but the most important one was that given in his speech by the spokesman for the People's Group, Mr Hernando. I am not against renewables - how am I going to be? - and what I am against is that they have a profitability of between 20% and 25%. I recommend that you read, because it is very illustrative, an article that I used here on one occasion - I am not going to go into detail now - of the former Minister of Industry, Mr Sebastián, because I think it is good that we do not act with prejudice, but that we really know what has happened. When someone knows a subject and knows that he has made mistakes and has the intellectual decency to publish it, he will not normally give bad advice to people who are willing to read his article, which I insist that I have read, seems very important to me, and they should all read it. I have to say that renewables today have a 7.5 % annual guarantee, which is not the 20 % or 25 % State guaranteed remuneration, but 7.5 % annually seems to me to be quite positive. I insist on what I told you before. He talks about the argument of legal certainty. The Supreme Court has given reason to the Government of Spain when it took decisions on this matter. I don't know what legal certainty will be like in our country. What I do know is that the latest data published by the OECD say that Spain is the country of the European Union that is receiving the most foreign investment in recent times. We are the ninth in the world, and from the western countries we are outnumbered by the United States, Canada and Australia. This is a positive fact, and it is also important that we all have it in our heads. He said that little progress had been made in the areas of fiscal policy, fiscal harmonisation and so on. The European Union is probably not going at the speed at which it should go. Then, referring to some other intervention, I will touch on this subject. We have been in favour of greater integration. The banking union has already been made, and it has been made in this period. When I came to the government, we had not started talking about the banking union, that is, that things are already being done. There is one very important issue: fiscal union, which is not going to be easy, because it affects nuclear aspects of the sovereignty of individual countries, and then there is the issue of political union. I agree that - as you said in your speech, I believe that Mr Esteban - what has happened, these debates that have taken place and that we have seen in public opinion, do not do much to help European political union. Finally, he also referred to the issue of the free trade treaty with the United States. I have already given my opinion before, I think it is an issue that can indeed be discussed, but I have already said where the main difficulties on the part of everyone are at present. He has also spoken in his economic intervention, and says that the exit from the crisis is the move to massive unemployment. I understand that you have to criticize the government, but as my colleague in the parliamentary group said, you should be a little more thoughtful of your statements, because that would also give you more credibility, believe me that I say this for your sake and not in a spirit different from what I have just mentioned in this part of my speech. There is an important fact, since you have talked about inequality: if you pick up indicator 80/20 - I have it here - or pick up Gini indicator or poverty rate indicator - all are the figures published by Eurostat - the years 2009, 2010, 2011 and 2012 were very bad, but 2013 we improved. In the case of indicator 80/20 we are already better than in 2009; in the case of the Gini indicator we are already better than in 2009, and in the case of the poverty rate we are already as in 2009. This does not satisfy me, but it is clear that the change in trend has taken place, and that some of the decisions that are being made are fortunately having beneficial effects for the citizens as a whole. It is important, and this is the end of the renewable energy section, that all honourable Members know that at this time the bulk of the energy being consumed in Spain is renewable energy. It is also increasing compared to 2012, that is, that one thing is that they have fewer premiums, and another is that they increase. The first is renewable energy, almost 50%, and the second is nuclear energy, 20%. We are in favour, in fact, of improving the use of renewables in our time. What we want are interconnections that lower the price of light and have no tariff deficit. You come up here and always talk about multinationals; we have nothing to do with any multinational, we have had problems - and everyone knows it - with the electricity companies, we have tried to explain our position to them, they have criticized us and they are in their right if they think that what we do is not right, but we are to defend general interests. I insist, general interests of all citizens, and not even partisan interests, let alone those to which you refer in your intervention. Mr Gorriarán says that I have not spoken about European economic policy. He says that some positive data observed in Spanish policy are fundamentally - as Mr Esteban also says in his speech - a consequence of European decisions, and not of Spanish decisions. I cannot agree with that statement, and I have also explained it on numerous occasions in this very rostrum. We propose a policy based on five points: firstly, fiscal consolidation in Spain; secondly, structural reforms in Spain; and thirdly, fourthly and fifthly, Europe. Much progress has been made in economic reforms in economic union; integration, banking union has been made, and liquidity, it is true that the European Central Bank has now taken decisions that I would have liked to have already taken in 2012. But if all this had occurred only by European decisions someone would have to explain to me why Spain is growing much more than the rest of the great countries of the European Union, with the exception of Germany. That is to say, what has been done in our country has undoubtedly been very useful to avoid much of the problems, and to ensure that we are now in a different situation. I repeat the arguments. You have also referred to this issue on the subject of Greece. He also spoke of poverty, inequality and unemployment. He talked about Tunisia. I agree. Tunisia needs special attention, because it is a country that has been modelled on its territory, has been able to make democratic elections with an unappealable result. ParlaMint-ES_2015-04-15-CD150415.u121	Thank you, Mr. González Cabaña. Minister.
ParlaMint-ES_2015-04-15-CD150415.u122	Thank you, Mr. President. We consider it a fundamental project for the economic development of the Bay of Cadiz and no one at any time has questioned the continuity of it. What the Government has done in recent weeks is to try to speed up the grant of the €60 million aid with which the project will be subsidized through the Feder funds. Since this project exceeds EUR 50 million in total cost, it requires the express approval of the European Commission, which during the investigation phase has made several requests for information to Spain that have been duly addressed. The Commission recently asked Spain for further updated information to be included in the application for co-financing. In the view of the Community authorities, the presentation of the new version of the project with this additional information requires a new KIC number for its processing. They therefore called for the temporary withdrawal of the application only in order to replace it with a new one. Therefore, this is a purely technical requirement, we could say that bureaucratic, which does not affect the present or the future of the project at all. The Spanish administration considers that this procedure is not really necessary, but we do not try to enter into a debate with the European Commission on the procedure, we comply with its requirements and we have therefore chosen the method of speeding up the deadlines and that the European Commission can approve the aid as soon as possible. Thank you very much, Mr President. [[Applauds]]

ParlaMint-ES_2015-04-15-CD150415.u179	Thank you, Mr. Paramo. Minister of Agriculture.
ParlaMint-ES_2015-04-15-CD150415.u180	Thank you, Your Honor. The local reform adopted through the Local Government Rationalization and Sustainability Act, which came into force in 2013, has not introduced any new developments in the management of beaches and bathing areas. In the new framework established by this law, the land public-sea domain continues to be protected within the competence of the municipalities, as already established by the Local Law of 1985, and among these powers are many others such as urban planning, management, implementation and discipline, parks and gardens, paving of public roads, preservation of roads and rural roads, local police, civil protection, fire prevention and extinguishment or protection of public health. For its part, the Costas Act of 1988 specifies and complements the competences which, as their own, the municipalities exercise within the maritime-terrestrial public domain and in its adjacent areas, in accordance with the Local Law. The reform of the Coast Law promoted by this Government does not bring about any change in this regard. Thus, article 115 establishes that the municipal jurisdiction is to maintain the beaches and public bathing places in the proper conditions of cleanliness, hygiene and health, as well as to monitor compliance with the rules and instructions on the rescue and safety of human roads. For its part, under the Constitution, the State must ensure the integrity of the public domain, including beaches and bathing areas, and ensure its free and free public use. But the specific competences in the area of the coast that depend on the characteristics of this physical space are specific to the municipalities, that is, they are not exercised by delegation of the State Administration. And, as your honor knows, all competence also requires the provision of means for its exercise. Article 142 of the Constitution establishes the principle of financial sufficiency of local entities. In addition, coastal municipalities have revenues derived from beach management. Your Honor, coastal tourist municipalities continue to count on the full cooperation of the Government for the protection of their beaches and bathing areas. Local reform does not entail any change in the management of these areas, nor does it therefore present any aggravation. In any event, the Government will consider any changes to the autonomous and local funding system that may arise. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-ES_2015-04-15-CD150415.u201	Thank you, Madam President. There are currently thousands of trade agreements in force that directly result in the loss of sovereignty, we know that, but this is not comparable to any other. In no way is an agreement on the import or export of goods, the TTIP or Transatlantic Agreement for Trade and Investment is the highest exponent of the current economic system. Changing the laws to use them against citizenship and in favor of big companies is to delve into the model that we so well know of the cuts and misery of the majority for the wealth of a few, but this is to delve brutally, so that if it were adopted in the terms we know we could talk about a before and after the TTIP, a new world order so desired by a few. Its recognized objective is - and I quote text - to eliminate the different situations and regulations that may exist between the two parties and thus achieve the unification of the markets, but always lowering, reducing rights, always matching from below. It intends to fly over European and state legislation, but always in one direction, that of the benefit of the big corporations, which, like Pedro for his home, will be able to counter, attack and denounce any rule that could harm his business, as has been acknowledged - and I quote again - that the laws of a state and the decisions of its governments never harm the big multinationals and their economic interests. In short, this agreement would put an end to the sovereignty of peoples, to democracy itself and to the capacity to legislate their institutions, which is essential for guaranteeing public rights and services for citizens. States, municipalities and self-governments would lose the little sovereignty we have. It is a real suicide to compare the US macro-farms and their model, without health or environmental conditions, with European sizes. And what is really serious is that the concept of food production and health security is absolutely opposed between the United States and Europe. In Europe we say: from the farm to the table, that is, healthy agricultural production is a healthy food. This requires a principle of prevention, with strict health regulations, which we know continue to favour the interests of industry against local production, but they do require, for example in meat, the conditioning of livestock premises, their food and water conditions, traceability throughout the chain. It is found that when risk elements are introduced without ensuring the precautionary principle in production, serious health crises have occurred. Do you remember the mad cow crisis? Well, it came about using meatmeal. In the United States, in addition to the use in the production of hormone meats, antibiotics, transgenics, etc., there is no prior control. Once the product is put on sale, if someone believes it is unhealthy they should prove it. By not being able to control the production process, public bodies cannot certify that the product is healthy and scientific certificates are made by companies, so they are always favorable. That's exactly what happens to transgenics in the United States. There 70% of the products are genetically modified. These products will flood our supermarkets and the worst part is that we will not be able to impose any limitations or develop any policy that will defend us from that pollution. That is, if an agreement adopted democratically by a State allegedly harmed the economy of an international corporation, it could resort directly to the international arbitration tribunals, which would resolve the dispute over the administration of justice in that country. That is to say, the sovereign power of the States disappears and it remains in the hands of three international judges to solve only the costs motivated by the agreement adopted and the fine, which must be paid by them. ParlaMint-ES_2015-04-15-CD150415.u204	Thank you, Mrs. Ariztegui. Minister for Agriculture, Food and Environment.
ParlaMint-ES_2015-04-15-CD150415.u205	Thank you very much, President. Mr President, reaching an ambitious and satisfactory free trade agreement between the United States and the European Union on both sides will undoubtedly promote economic growth on both sides of the Atlantic and will have a positive impact on our economy and on job creation, i.e. on the Spanish. The United States is a very popular market for many sectors of our economy and presents great opportunities for our agri-food sector, and our agri-food sector is asking us to do so. The Government in these negotiations monitors and defends the interests of all our sectors in order to achieve an ambitious and balanced treaty. The European Union is the world's leading trading power with more than 500 million inhabitants. It imports more goods and services than the United States or China. Foreign trade employs more than 30 million people in the European Union, of whom about one and a half million are Spanish citizens. The European Union and the United States as a whole, with more than 800 million inhabitants, are the world's largest trading and investment partners. Together we produce more than 50% of the world's gross domestic product, generating one third of the world's trade. The European Union is the second exporter of goods to the United States, only behind China, and is also the second most important destination for its exports, only behind Canada. As for Spain, the United States is a trading partner of strategic and fundamental importance to our economy. Our exports to the United States reached 10,642 million euros in 2014. This is a higher figure than all exports to Argentina, Brazil and Mexico, the three main trading partners in Ibero-America. As far as agri-food trade is concerned, it is a key market for Spain, and that is what our sector is repeatedly asking for. Of the total Spanish agri-food exports - EUR 40,830 million in 2014-, 24,2 % is destined for third countries; of that percentage, 14 % is allocated to the United States. It is the first market between third countries and represents 3.4% of our total exports. This meant a total of EUR 1,383 million last year. These figures reflect the high degree of integration that is already taking place between the two economies, but there is no doubt that there is an important potential for improvement and development, which is what is being pursued in the current negotiations, which will culminate in a negotiating process tried on previous occasions without satisfactory progress. A transatlantic council was established in 2007 to give impetus to the transatlantic economy. The high-level group established at this forum in February 2013, in the context of an economic, financial and growth crisis in both areas of the Atlantic, recommended that the procedures for negotiating a free trade area be initiated with the conviction of both parties that reaching this agreement would have a very beneficial effect on economic growth and employment. For Spain this agreement will mean increasing the volume of our exports and continuing to give our agri-food sector new opportunities for growth, development and job creation throughout the territory, a priority and irrefutable objective of this government. This context of economic crisis has made the agreement go beyond the classic approach of tariff reduction and market opening, including aspects such as regulatory convergence or technical barriers, which does not mean elimination of regulation. All public or private studies agree that this agreement will have significant benefits for both economies and will have a positive impact on the global economy as a whole. The Centre for Economic Policy Research's impact analysis for the European Commission estimates an increase of 0.5% in the Community's gross domestic product and 0.4% in the United States, an increase in Community exports of 187 billion and an average increase in disposable income of EUR 545 per family: welfare for citizens. According to the Bertelsmann Foundation study, Spain would be the fourth country that would benefit most from job creation, generating between 36,000 and 143,000 new jobs depending on whether a tariff-limited agreement or a broader agreement was reached, also addressing non-tariff barriers. As far as per capita income is concerned, it attributes Spain a growth of between 0.31% and 6.55% according to these different scenarios, and in both cases the benefits for our country will be above the Community average. Thus, great opportunities are opening up for the European economy and for the Spanish economy, and particularly good for our agri-food sector. In June 2013, the Council of the European Union unanimously approved the negotiating mandate for the Commission. The agreement as a whole is structured into three pillars: market access, regulatory aspects and non-tariff barriers, and standards. Market access negotiates as wide a tariff reduction as possible on the basis of tariffs that are generally already low by both parties. Some tariff peaks still persist, such as for tuna canned fish, where, according to estimates by the sector itself, tariff elimination would result in the creation of 35,000 new jobs in the canned fish sector in Spain. This broad tariff reduction does not exclude a differentiated treatment for the most sensitive products in both areas, which may consist of more prolonged tariff reductions over time, the establishment of quotas or the inclusion of a bilateral safeguard clause in the event of threat or disruption of markets. The second pillar, on regulatory aspects and non-tariff barriers, is even more important than the tariff chapter. This includes an essential aspect for the agri-food sector: sanitary or phytosanitary measures. In this chapter it is where the greatest benefits of the agreement can be achieved. The aim is to remove unnecessary barriers to trade and to promote the compatibility of legislation through mutual recognition, which is not removed. The aim is to intensify bilateral dialogue to build the necessary confidence between the parties, increase transparency, reduce trade barriers and improve cooperation on issues related to technical barriers to trade, all of which will facilitate openness to transparency and convergence of regulatory approaches. The objective is to reduce redundant certification requirements, to promote the confidence of the certification bodies of both parties and, ultimately, to enhance trade and development. Removing these barriers is essential for some of our products. For example, for olives, in some cases it is mandatory to carry out inspections in 100 per cent of shipments, which is an additional cost and a serious difficulty in accessing the US market. Nevertheless, Spanish table olive exports account for an average of 19% of our total exports over the past six years, reaching over 350,000 tonnes. How much better it would be if these unnecessary barriers were removed. The removal of such barriers has therefore been raised by the Spanish delegation to the Commission, which has taken it as its own in the negotiations. Another clear example of these difficulties which are now to be overcome affects clearly exporting sectors, such as fruit and vegetables. For this sector the American authorization procedures are extremely slow, fourteen years in the case of avocados and apricots. This reduces commercial interest when the United States completes the authorization process and loses development opportunities. In many cases the export conditions limit our potential exporter because of its high cost, such as the requirement of the presence of an American inspector during the export campaign paid at the expense of the Spanish exporters. All this is what is intended to improve. The third pillar of the agreement is the rules, and it includes an essential and extremely important element for our interests, that of the protection of geographical indications. Our goal is to achieve a clear improvement in the protection of our quality figures. In 2006 the United States and the European Union signed a bilateral wine trade agreement recognizing the protection of Community designations of origin. This agreement was an important step forward, but it does not guarantee exclusivity for a number of designations of origin, including Sherry or Malaga, if used before the date of entry into force. This is one of the elements that we want to improve in the current agreement by returning the exclusivity in the use of names to their legitimate owners. Your Honor, this government has worked intensively over the past three years to strengthen the internationalization of our companies, so that sectors, including agri-food, grow and create jobs, to increase our presence in traditional markets and to search for new ones, because food industry exports reached 40,829 million euros in 2014. We are the eighth world food-exporting country; it is a priority objective of this Government to consolidate and improve our presence in all international markets. The World Trade Organization accounts for 90% of world economic growth in the coming years outside the European Union, so we are convinced that more and more markets and more products need to be reached. It is an objective of this government to export more volume with greater added value, and for a clear reason: the agri-food sector already accounts for about 30% of production abroad; it accounts for between 30% and 40% of total employment, about five hundred thousand jobs, and this can increase. That is why we established a strategic plan for internationalization, within which, in May 2013, practically coincided with the launch of the TTIP negotiations, strategic lines for internationalization were adopted. We are convinced that there is a capacity in Spain to reach higher market shares, both by improving our presence in traditional markets and by consolidating and expanding new markets. The U.S. is one of our target markets, because it will bring opportunities for growth and employment in rural areas, and that's what we're looking for. However, as is inevitable in any trade agreement, there are sensitivities in the different chapters of the negotiation that must be protected and where more vigilance is needed. We are aware that any concession that does not consider the substantial differences in the conditions of production between the European Union and the United States could cause injury to producers, which have to comply with stricter rules and which entails higher production costs. But we are fully convinced of the opportunities for a satisfactory outcome. That is why the Government has supported a comprehensive and ambitious agreement, which does not mean supporting any agreement. As far as the agri-food sector is concerned, the European Union has, over time, been providing itself with a series of conditional provisions that constitute the European agricultural model. It is true that this model poses greater demands for our producers. These are the standards of quality and safety which European society demands and which we are not prepared to give up. Let me stress that we are faced with the most important trade negotiations in the European Union so far. As Commissioner Malmström said in this House on 6 March, the TTIP agreement, in addition to helping to establish the largest free trade area in the world, with all that it entails in terms of growth, development and employment for both parties, will remove bureaucratic obstacles, simplify customs procedures and serve to confirm other common values, such as those relating to human rights, fundamental freedoms and citizen security. Thank you very much. [[Applauds]]

As for your assertion that this is an agreement that is being conducted in secret, without transparency and without civil society participation, I would like to say that this is not true. Before the current negotiations began, there was a long process of reflection which included a public consultation together with various debates in the Council and the European Parliament, where the whole of European citizenship is represented. This decision, as you see, was not made in secret; neither are the negotiations. I invite everyone to visit the Commission's website to check the information and all the documentation available in connection with this matter. A major information campaign is also being carried out by the Government of Spain together with the European Commission, with a website where extensive information is available. On the part of the Ministry of Agriculture, we maintain permanent contacts with the representatives of the sector in order to know their views, interests and concerns and to establish their positions on the basis of them. These are the ones who ask us to move on. There is a Trade-related Consultative Commission on International Trade Negotiations in which all productive sectors, including agricultural professional organizations, are represented, which meets at least twice a year and where the status of trade agreements is informed on time. In the Ministry, in the Working Group on Internationalization, we have set up a specific working group to inform all agri-food sectors on time. The European Union foresees among its objectives for negotiation that the agreement promotes high levels of protection for the environment, work and consumers. The European Union has committed itself in the negotiating mandate to ensuring that trade and investment growth is achieved without reducing the application of standards and policies of Member States, on vital issues such as sustainable development, employment and the environment that you see as being so threatened, and not so. TTIP will not result in a decrease in the level of consumer protection, inter alia, because both parties have a very high level of protection. The general idea of this treaty is to establish mechanisms to facilitate trade, to establish regulatory cooperation that allows, to the extent possible, to avoid divergences between the regulatory approach adopted by each of the parties, so that new trade barriers do not arise - they are commercial but not protective neither of the environment nor of the safety of the citizens - and therefore that they do not in any case mean a reduction in levels of safety and health that each party is responsible for in its territory. The United States and the European Union have very high labour, social and environmental standards, of the best in the world. The agreement will not reduce these standards, it will not contain provisions that call into question the European Union's ability to regulate its labour market, social and environmental standards. The European Union - it has already done so in many other trade agreements already concluded - has proposed the inclusion of provisions on trade and sustainable development to ensure that the increase in trade does not occur at the expense of labour rights or the environment, and the negotiation of this agreement will not be an exception in this regard. Social labour or environmental policies will therefore simply not be affected or affected. The mandate of the negotiation implies that the application of environmental, employment and sustainable development standards should not be reduced. On the part of the European Union's sanitary and phytosanitary measures, we in the European Union envisage the prohibition of the use of antibiotics that promote growth, the use of hormones or the import of animals that have been fed with them or restrictions on the cultivation and import of GMOs. They are practices that are applicable in the United States and, therefore, these issues will receive appropriate treatment, and the Commission has repeatedly stated this. The aim is to remove trade barriers, without reducing safety and health levels. This is the position of the Government and this is what the European Commission guarantees us, who, at a meeting in the Joint Committee for the European Union and for Economics and Competitiveness on 6 March last, mentioned that TTIP will not undermine our levels of protection of the food safety environment. We will only cooperate with the United States on regulatory issues where we have the same objectives. We are not going to change the rules on hormones in meat or on genetically modified organisms. These are not my words; they are the words of Commissioner Malmström, responsible for the negotiation, just over fifteen days ago, and I believe that things cannot be said in a clearer way. Ladies and gentlemen, as I see there is little time left for me to conclude by saying that the Spanish Government certainly supports such negotiations, because it can represent a historic opportunity for the Spanish economy, especially for the agri-food sector for which we have been working for more than three years. ETA. This explicit threat clearly sums up the macabre objective of the terrorists, a threat which they carried out by implementing a programming of murders which precisely sought the elimination of their political-ideological opponents or their abandonment from the Basque Country or from Navarre, in which, logically and in both cases, the families of the victims, killed or not, were forced to leave their own land and try to start a new life elsewhere, often lacking the necessary resources. An article by lawyer Miguel Ángel Iglesias, published on April 9, collected a number of the macabre claims with which terrorists sought to justify their criminal actions. These various demands had as their common factor the elimination of the political adversary, who was killed not because he had abandoned his people or his land before the consideration of ETA, in his own delusional justification, to classify the non-nationalist Basques as a sub-concept of the Basque people and to consider that, as such, they should not continue living in the Basque Country, but because it was necessary to eliminate, I insist, those who opposed what they intended, which was to impose, I repeat, their totalitarian and exclusionary project through socialization, that is, the generalization of suffering and terror to all those who opposed what they intended. I believe, however, ladies and gentlemen, that today, 15 April 2015, we can say that, despite the immense pain and suffering caused, they have not achieved their objectives. The Basque Country continues to be institutionalized in the Autonomous Community of the Basque Country, Navarre remains the Foral Community of Navarre and Spain remains Spain, a democratic and legal State of which, together with other autonomous communities and cities, the Autonomous Community of the Basque Country and the Foral Community of Navarre, and both with their historical, political, institutional, specific and differentiated identity. And they have failed to do so thanks to the strength of Spanish society and the rule of law as a whole, with all the powers, thanks to international cooperation, to the ethical example of the victims, who never fell into the temptation to take justice by their hand, precisely because they trusted that the rule of law would do justice to them. I have a duty to remember that at the forefront of the struggle, which has triumphed, of the democratic and right-wing State to defeat these terrorists have been the State Security Forces and Corpses, the Civil Guard and the National Police. Three hundred and fifty civilian guards and police have died killed in that battle, and I do not count the wounded, the injured or the enormous material damage they have caused, but three hundred and fifty civilian guards and national police - dead, murdered, I repeat - have given their lives so that today we can enjoy our freedom. Now that we have policely defeated ETA we have many battles ahead, we are not finished. We have won great battles; war - let me express it - we have not won it yet. The battle of the story is the battle that opens now and we are facing it with the same determination with which we have fought against terrorist barbarism. He knows very well that the battle of the story is fundamental. We must preserve the memory, the dignity, the justice, the historical truth of what happened in the face of interested stories that have nothing to do with the interested ones who are promoting the memory of forgetfulness, surely because if they looked face to face at the truth of what happened they would have to lower their sight; we must preserve the truth of the story of what happened without equidistance between victims and murderers and with the ethical reference of the victims as our breath and our impulse to achieve it. In this line of considerations, Your Honor, the consideration as genocide of ETA barbarities will clearly contribute to putting terrorists in their historic place and also, as I said, those who vittorated them and even supported their actions. I began by pointing out, as your honor, the institutional declaration of the Foral Parliament of Navarre. It will be a day when freedom will have triumphed, on which the historical truth will have triumphed, on which the Democrats will have triumphed and the murderers and those who have thrown them out for too many years will have been defeated. ParlaMint-ES_2015-04-15-CD150415.u229	Thank you, Mr. President. Ladies and gentlemen, the motion put forward by Mr Centella on behalf of the Plural Left allows me to analyse the situation of the Spanish bases and, more specifically, the situation at the base of Morón and the change in use, functions and nature that the United States Administration is currently demanding. After two years of temporary authorizations by the Government of Spain to comply with the request of the Department of State for Security to increase the number of troops and aircraft for new missions in North Africa, the United States Administration requests to make this situation permanent and to expand its functions. Ladies and gentlemen, progress that we do not share at all the general principles on which the motion we are debating is based, which calls for the denunciation of the bilateral agreement between Spain and the United States and the closure of the Spanish bases for joint use, since we believe that they are an essential instrument within our country's security and defence strategy, with our integration into a global security system and our membership of NATO, especially in view of the growing instability and cross-border threats in North Africa and the Middle East. That is why we have tabled an amendment calling for information and transparency from the Government on the open negotiations with the United States for the creation of a new protocol in the current bilateral convention. Firstly, the establishment and definition of Spain's needs and interests when authorising the new uses of the Morón base, so that these are guaranteed in the new protocol. Secondly, we consider it essential that, in the decisions to be taken in the face of a crisis situation, Spain and the United States should act as partners and avoid unilateralism, since that would mean a very clear regression in the Spanish autonomy and sovereignty enshrined in the 1988 Convention on Cooperation for Defence, which placed Spain on an equal and respectful footing, transforming what had hitherto been subordinate relations into relations between allies based on trust and the common interests of the two countries. Thirdly, the inexcusable demand that these negotiations and the agreements to be reached should ensure the permanence and stability of the jobs and services traditionally carried out by civilian workers at the base of Morón since 1972. In recent years, 240 workers have been laid off by being replaced by US personnel as the strength and tasks and services at the base increased. Ladies and gentlemen, in April 2013, the Government authorized the temporary one-year deployment of 500 marines and eight aircraft to respond to rapid operations in response to crisis situations in Africa following the serious attacks of Benghazi and instability in the Sahel, Maghreb and the new field of action of jihadist terrorism. The following year, in April 2014, the Government of Spain was again requested to increase its strength and to extend for an additional year the increase and presence of up to 1,100 marines and 10 aircraft. The authorization of the Government of Spain no longer referred to specific actions, but - I read literally - to the new security challenges on the African continent, which require maximum cooperation in order to maintain the security of Spain and the United States as a contribution to international security and the maintenance of peace. The authorization of deployment would - the Council of Ministers agreed - allow it to be used for security cooperation missions in Africa. But, ladies and gentlemen, neither in 2013 nor 2014 - although we already saw the need for the continuation of these deployments - the Minister of Defence was pleased to report to Congress, despite the repeated requests of our parliamentary group. Ladies and gentlemen, the reality we have today is that the base of Morón is, from a strategic point of view for the United States, an African base together with Djibouti and Niger and its strategic value in the face of new threats in Africa increases once the United States has definitively ruled out the installation of its own base in Morocco. In December 2014, it is the last request, the United States Government requests Spain to grant the indefinite nature of the new role of the base and the increase in strength. On 23 January, the Council of Ministers authorised the opening of negotiations for the amendment of the convention for the indefinite deployment of a military force at the base of Moron. Mr President, our amendment raises, first of all, the need for the House to know the terms of negotiation between the two administrations for the amendment of the convention. In addition to the informal consultations that may have taken place, we believe that, prior to the signing of the amendment protocol, the Foreign and Defence Ministers must appear together to explain the terms of the negotiation and the possible agreement. This new protocol must have the greatest possible consensus in the House, and for this, as I said earlier, greater transparency, information and negotiation is necessary. Secondly, we believe that the new protocol should clearly contemplate the authorized uses of the base without at any time implying a loss of Spanish sovereignty and genuinely responding to the national interests of our country for its security and defence. Finally, we consider it inexcusable that in the negotiation and signing of the convention the permanence of jobs is agreed, as I said earlier. The increase of the new troops requested can be expected to reach the 3,000 marines resulting from this deployment of a permanent force, which must also translate into economic and labour benefits for the Spanish workers and for the peoples of the region of Morón, in Seville: Arahal, the Viso del Alcor, Morón de la Frontera, El Coronil and Alcalá de Guadaira.

This agreement, which is of 1988 between Spain and the United States, is undergoing a revision. The Government, because of the inability to negotiate, because of the complexity of the negotiation, has recently agreed in the Council of Ministers to extend it for a further year to negotiate a new protocol to the 1988 Convention. But this is an agreement that defines the presence of allied bases, obviously with the co-responsibility of the host country and of the United States, which can be clearly in the future - and this is on the table and is part of the discussion - in Spain, Italy or Morocco, as has been pointed out and as it is in the arrangement. And it does not seem to us negative, according to what was discussed this Monday in Barcelona at the anti-jihadist summit, which welcomed up to 39 foreign ministers from the northern and southern shores of the Mediterranean, that this base - which is also a basis to combat jihadism - is in Spain and not in Italy or Morocco. Ladies and gentlemen, I am not telling you that we are putting forward an attitude of unconditional support, because there have been mistakes and some mistakes of the past explain the situation of the present. The US intervention in Iraq in 2003 helps explain many of the basic mistakes and phenomena of the present, and the Libyan error, in which Britain participated and a foreign policy that is not usually wrong in international politics such as that of France, is also paid. Therefore, I am not going to paint you a perfect world, but I am going to tell you that in the face of the third worldism in foreign policy and defence that you pose to us or the renewed commitment to NATO, we are in favour of the latter. Mr Centella, we are also people of pact and dialogue and we are going to vote in favour of point 5 because we are in favour of the labour rights of Andalusian citizens, who have to have a decent economic impact on the development of the foundations. But it doesn't seem like a good argument to me what you said when you talked about making sure the Andalusians are not mistaken, the United States is no longer rich. Does this mean that if there was an economic benefit, you would be in favour of NATO and politics with the United States? I think we are discussing principles and not economic revenues in international relations. Nothing more and thank you very much, Mr President.
